Independent reauthorization: Resumption or expansion after pause, narrowing, or abort requires a currently authenticated Decision 136 trigger; restored continuity as applicable; current Canadian authority; a fresh life-safety assessment; a specific RCMP incident-command decision; contemporaneous documentation of the changed condition; and confirmation that the proposed action is the least intrusive effective response. An earlier trigger cannot be banked after material change.

Roles: The RCMP incident commander alone orders pause, narrowing, abort, resumption, or expansion. Julie reports provenance, identity, continuity, and alternative-explanation defects but does not command the field response. Elias reports technical ambiguity, package-signature failure, automation, decoy behavior, or inconsistency and may not use unofficial access to restore confidence. United States personnel may corroborate on request but cannot veto a Canadian abort, order resumption, or create cross-border action.

Narrative/structural lock: The climax proceeds as a controlled sequence of trigger, bounded intervention, material change, mandatory reassessment, pause or narrowing, possible fresh reauthorization, and final outcome. The operation may change shape without granting institutional omniscience or unlimited authority.

Procedural/jurisdictional lock: Continuous legal, evidentiary, technical, tactical, and life-safety reporting is required. Every continuation remains Canadian and bounded. Decision 137 does not authorize package opening or exploitation, establish the final arrest basis, define force, merge national repositories, or create informal hot pursuit.

Character lock: Julie must stop or limit an operation she helped justify when the evidentiary basis changes. Elias must expose uncertainty even when doing so threatens recovery.

Suspense lock: Surrender, decoy exposure, package inactivity, civilian movement, medical crisis, technical contradiction, environmental change, or approach to a territorial boundary can compel the RCMP to change or end the intervention while the objective remains within reach.

Alternative-explanation lock: Sensor loss, shielding, genuine or staged emergencies, tactical delay, lawful hazard avoidance, and innocent movement remain testable possibilities. Uncertainty is not treated as either innocence or hostile intent.

Proof ceiling: Satisfaction of an abort or narrowing condition requires the RCMP incident commander to pause, terminate, or reduce the intervention to the smallest remaining lawful and necessary action, and any renewed or expanded intervention requires fresh authorization on current evidence and authority. It does not prove that the original trigger was mistaken, that the commander or package was misidentified, that no offense occurred, that the package was harmless, or that later action is forbidden if a new lawful trigger is authenticated.

## Forward target: Decision 138 — recovery, stabilization, custody, and examination boundaries for the incomplete portable package.Decision 138 — The Incomplete Portable Package Uses Phased Canadian-Controlled Stabilization, Sealed Custody, and Warrant-Gated Forensic Examination

Selection: D.

Status: Locked.

Locked architecture

The incomplete portable package is recovered and handled through four strictly separated Canadian-controlled phases. Physical possession does not itself authorize technical exploitation, cross-border access, or a complete attribution conclusion.

Phase 1 — Immediate safety stabilization

A specialized Canadian team acts under the RCMP incident commander solely to protect life, prevent immediate evidence loss, and determine whether the package can be moved.

Before contact, the team creates a complete visual and video record of the package’s location, orientation, visible connections, indicators, power state, displays, cables, accessories, damage, seals, environmental conditions, and nearby devices.

The team tests for specific electrical, thermal, explosive, chemical, transmission, remote-access, and destructive-data risks. It may use shielding, physical separation, cooling, controlled power preservation, or controlled power termination only when an observed risk justifies the action.

The least intrusive effective stabilization method governs. Every action, operator, tool, time, observation, and resulting change must be recorded.

No content search, credential use, exploratory opening, general forensic acquisition, or intelligence exploitation is permitted merely because the package has been recovered.

A narrowly necessary opening is permitted only when competent Canadian specialists determine that opening is required to address a specific immediate hazard or prevent imminent irreversible evidence destruction. Continuous recording is required, and the action stops when the narrow purpose is satisfied.

Phase 2 — Evidentiary recovery and sealed Canadian custody

After stabilization, the RCMP establishes a formal package-custody record containing:

• exact recovery time and location;

• the identity and role of every person entering the recovery zone;

• pre-contact, stabilization, and post-stabilization condition;

• visible serial numbers, labels, tamper indicators, damage, alterations, and modifications;

• an inventory of cables, media, modules, tools, containers, notes, and associated devices;

• every change caused by stabilization;

• separate packaging and identifiers for separable components when appropriate;

• tamper-evident seals and unique evidence numbers;

• transport conditions, environmental controls, and transfer signatures;

• continuous delivery to a secure Canadian evidence repository.

The original package remains in Canadian custody. It is not informally transferred to Julie, Elias, a United States agency, or a joint mobile team.

Phase 3 — Separately authorized, preservation-first forensic examination

Technical examination begins only after competent Canadian authority approves a written protocol identifying the object, purpose, questions, methods, participants, tools, permitted scope, stopping conditions, and required records.

The examination sequence is:

1. verify the seals and custody ledger;

2. document the package again before opening or connection;

3. determine whether non-invasive methods can answer the approved question;

4. create a validated forensic acquisition when technically possible;

5. preserve the original and analyze verified working copies;

6. record tools, versions, commands, credentials, timestamps, errors, and every induced change;

7. distinguish direct observations from interpretations and hypotheses;

8. stop and seek expanded authority when materially different data, devices, persons, or purposes fall outside the approved scope;

9. preserve encrypted, unreadable, damaged, excluded, or technically inaccessible material instead of improvising unsupported access.

The package’s incomplete nature remains controlling. Examiners may not assume that it is safe, functional, comprehensive, authentic in every component, or self-explanatory.

Phase 4 — Bounded expert and cross-border access

Julie may examine authorized provenance outputs, custody records, manifests, logs, and derivative forensic results necessary to test the investigation’s claims. She does not personally seize, open, connect to, alter, or retain the original package.

Elias may serve only as a formally documented and supervised subject-matter expert. He may identify architecture he genuinely recognizes, explain expected behavior, distinguish known components from anomalies, identify technical risks created by proposed actions, and assess whether results are consistent with authentic system behavior.

Elias may not use private or undeclared credentials, reconnect the package to an external system, perform unsupervised access, conceal a method from the forensic record, become the sole witness to a critical result, remove or retain package data, or override the Canadian examiner.

United States investigators receive only lawfully approved derivative material, reports, or forensic copies. The Canadian original, custody ledger, examination record, and repository remain distinct from the United States evidence chain.

Controlling distinctions

Stabilization protects life, prevents immediate loss, and makes safe transport possible.

Seizure and custody establish lawful possession and preservation.

Forensic acquisition creates a controlled evidentiary representation.

Examination answers authorized technical questions.

Exploitation uses the package or its contents for intelligence or operational purposes and requires separate authority.

Disclosure or sharing transfers approved information and does not transfer ownership or custody of the original evidence.

Authority for one category does not automatically authorize the next.

Operational and narrative consequences

The package may be physically recovered during the climax without becoming a magical container that proves the entire conspiracy. A limited stabilization problem can preserve immediate danger while deeper examination remains a disciplined post-climax investigation.

External observations or a narrow stabilization action may answer one urgent question. Later examination may produce consequential but bounded findings, including evidence that the package is active, shielded, damaged, partly erased, missing a component, connected to an unknown accessory, assembled from mixed-origin components, or configured in a way Elias does not recognize.

Missing or unreadable data may result from incompleteness, damage, ordinary security controls, prior removal, failed acquisition, or deliberate deletion. An active process may be benign maintenance, preservation, automation, defensive action, or hostile conduct. A recognizable component may have been copied, substituted, or repurposed by another actor.

Canada retains original custody and examination authority. United States support remains requested and derivative. The national repositories, disclosure decisions, and evidence chains do not merge.

Julie gains access to evidence needed for provenance analysis without becoming its custodian. Elias contributes through transparent expertise rather than privileged access, preserving his independence and his eventual move to the scientist’s independent audit laboratory.

The 100,000-word hard minimum and the 105,000–112,000-word planning target remain unchanged.

Proof ceiling: Recovery and phased examination establish Canadian custody of the documented package, the package’s condition at recovery, the actions taken to stabilize and preserve it, and the results produced within the authorized forensic protocol. They do not prove that the package is complete, that it was not previously copied or altered, that every component shares one origin, that the commander understood or intended every function, that it was operational before recovery, that it was intended for harmful use, or that it connects the commander to the Decision 131 parent actor or the Western Pacific chain.

Forward target: Decision 139 — surrender, apprehension, initial detention, and legal-status boundaries for the authenticated commander and any immediate custodians.

## Decision 139 — A Differentiated Individualized Custody Matrix Governs Surrender, Apprehension, Initial Detention, and Legal Status

Selection: D.

Status: Locked.

Core lock: The RCMP governs the authenticated commander and every immediate custodian through three distinct layers: surrender and immediate scene-safety control; individualized Canadian legal classification; and rights-protected custody, questioning, and transfer. No person’s status is derived automatically from another person’s status, package proximity, shared travel, employment, simultaneous surrender, or association with the commander.

Layer 1 — Surrender and immediate scene control

The RCMP establishes a visible, recorded surrender route that separates people from the incomplete portable package without continuing tactical escalation after the Decision 136 trigger has ceased.

Each person is instructed to stop all package and device interaction; place weapons, tools, credentials, communications equipment, access objects, and other directed items where officers can see them; move individually along the designated route; keep hands visible; avoid unsupervised communication with other persons; disclose injuries and immediate hazards; submit to the minimum safety search necessary for scene control; and remain separated until identity and immediate status are determined.

The RCMP records the instructions given; whether they could reasonably be heard or received; each person’s response; any delay, inability, resistance, confusion, or attempt to comply; each person’s location relative to the package; any object discarded, retained, concealed, or surrendered; and any spontaneous statement.

Effective surrender narrows or ends tactical escalation under Decision 137 when the triggering condition has ceased. It does not erase independently supported arrest grounds or authorize questioning beyond the person’s actual legal status.

A request for counsel, refusal to answer, invocation of legal rights, inability to understand instructions, medical impairment, fear, confusion, or delayed but genuine compliance is not treated by itself as hostile conduct or proof of guilt.

Layer 2 — Individualized Canadian legal classification

Once immediate safety is established, the RCMP assigns each person one current legal status supported by individualized evidence and competent Canadian authority. Status changes must be recorded when the evidentiary or legal basis changes.

Status A — Arrest

A person may be arrested only when competent Canadian officers identify grounds specific to that person. Relevant evidence may include authenticated identity; personally observed Decision 136 conduct; direct package possession or control; verified instructions personally issued or followed; attempted destruction, transfer, concealment, use, alteration, or removal; credible evidence of another independently recognized offense; or flight or resistance considered with, not instead of, the broader evidence.

The arrest record identifies the arresting authority, individualized grounds, arrest time, objects seized, the person’s condition and injuries, rights provided, and any later change in status.

The commander’s Decision 133 authentication is relevant to identity and attribution, but it does not by itself prove a particular offense or authorize unlimited custody, questioning, search, or package exploitation.

Status B — Bounded investigative or scene detention

A person may be temporarily detained only when Canadian law and current circumstances support that form of detention. The record must identify a defined reason, start time, limited purpose, review point, and clear release, arrest, or reclassification condition.

Investigative or scene detention cannot become an indefinite holding category while investigators search for evidence, await political direction, or attempt to convert association into individualized grounds.

Status C — Voluntary witness

A person for whom no custody basis exists may be asked to remain, provide identification, preserve records, or participate voluntarily in an interview. The record must state whether the person was free to leave, consented to the interview, consented to any search or production, requested counsel, or withdrew cooperation.

Useful information, technical knowledge, possession of records, or proximity to the commander does not convert voluntary cooperation into lawful detention.

Status D — Protective, medical, or safeguarding custody

A person may be protected, transported, separated, or medically controlled only when a distinct safety, medical, coercion, incapacity, or witness-protection basis exists.

This status may apply to a coerced courier, injured participant, hostage, threatened witness, person exposed to a package-associated hazard, or person unable to care for themselves during the emergency.

Protective or medical custody cannot be used to obtain investigative access, consent, statements, credentials, or evidence that arrest, a warrant, or genuinely voluntary cooperation would not permit.

Status E — Release

A person must be released when no adequate basis remains for arrest, detention, medical control, or protective custody.

Release may include lawful evidence-preservation instructions, contact requirements, or notice of future process. It cannot include disguised detention, informal surveillance presented as a release condition, or compelled cooperation unsupported by separate authority.

Layer 3 — Rights-protected custody, questioning, and transfer

Any person kept in Canadian custody receives the protections appropriate to the person’s actual status. The governing requirements include a prompt statement of status and grounds; access to counsel where required; interpretation or communication assistance; medical evaluation and treatment; recording of every status change; separation from the original package and its forensic examination; separate custody records for clothing, devices, access objects, and physical evidence; documented interview authority; and preservation of the distinction between voluntary statements and custodial questioning.

Investigators may not use asserted package danger to coerce cooperation, promise release in exchange for undocumented technical access, use Elias as an unofficial interrogator or credential broker, or invoke Julie’s analytical authority to imply that guilt has already been established.

Questioning and package examination remain separate evidentiary activities. Investigators may compare statements with Decision 138 forensic results, but an unverified statement cannot redefine package evidence without independent testing, and a forensic ambiguity cannot be converted into a confession demand.

Treatment of immediate custodians

Every immediate custodian is assessed separately. Possible roles remain open, including knowing accomplice, coerced courier, hired transporter unaware of the contents, technician assigned a narrow function, property owner, safety specialist, whistleblower, evidence-preservation intermediary, hostage, undercover or governmental actor, and unrelated person caught inside the perimeter.

Physical proximity, employment, shared travel, possession of a generic device, simultaneous surrender, or association with the commander is insufficient by itself to establish common purpose, package knowledge, harmful intent, or arrest grounds.

Treatment of the authenticated commander

Decision 133 establishes the commander’s natural-person identity only within its locked proof limits. It does not automatically establish criminal guilt, harmful intent, knowledge of every package component, package operability, control of the Western Pacific chain, a relationship with the Decision 131 parent actor, state sponsorship, common purpose with every custodian, or authorization of an attack.

The commander may be arrested when individualized Canadian grounds exist. The commander may also surrender, request counsel, remain silent, provide a bounded exculpatory account, claim protective or evidentiary purpose, identify another actor, or offer information that is partly true and partly deceptive.

Surrender reduces the lawful need for force but creates no immunity. Silence, refusal to confess, refusal to provide credentials, or refusal to cooperate creates no additional guilt.

Canadian and United States boundary

The RCMP retains physical custody, legal classification, interview control, medical and safeguarding decisions, and transfer authority in Canada.

United States investigators may provide authenticated evidence already lawfully shared, submit proposed questions through Canadian investigators, observe when Canadian authority permits, request evidence preservation, and seek later lawful access through the proper process.

United States personnel may not arrest or detain anyone in Canada; independently question a person in Canadian custody; threaten United States transfer to compel cooperation; condition medical care or Canadian release on cooperation; remove the commander or a custodian from Canada without lawful Canadian process; or convert the joint investigation into merged custody.

Any later transfer, extradition, immigration action, witness movement, or cross-border prosecution requires separate authority and does not arise automatically from this decision.

Julie’s role

Julie may explain the confidence and limits of commander authentication, identify evidence relevant to individualized status, distinguish personal conduct from system-level attribution, flag contradictions or alternative explanations, and help formulate provenance questions for authorized Canadian investigators.

Julie may not declare an arrest, conduct a custodial interrogation, promise release or protection, threaten package consequences, access protected evidence beyond her authorized role, or treat silence as confirmation.

Her climax test is whether she can support lawful custody without turning analytical confidence into personal judgment.

Elias’s role

Elias may identify technical roles suggested by observed conduct, distinguish commander-level behavior from routine technical handling, explain whether a custodian’s actions appear consistent with stabilization, activation, preservation, transmission, concealment, or destruction, and provide transparent expert assistance through the Canadian investigative process.

Elias may not interrogate the commander, negotiate credentials, privately test a suspect’s knowledge, offer technical immunity, enter an undocumented cooperation arrangement, or become the sole witness supporting a critical classification.

Narrative and structural function

The climax may resolve through full surrender, partial surrender, arrest, bounded detention, release, protective custody, medical custody, or mixed individualized outcomes rather than one collective result.

This creates a complete bridge from tactical climax to legal and investigative denouement. It prevents the ending from collapsing into either extreme: everyone walks away because the package remains incomplete, or everyone is treated as guilty because the RCMP operation succeeded.

A custodian’s classification may produce a consequential turn: one is arrested, one is revealed as coerced, one becomes a witness, one requires medical protection, one must be released despite political pressure, or one supplies a bounded explanation that changes the authorized Decision 138 examination.

Suspense remains available when one person complies while another reaches for a device; a custodian claims the commander is protecting the package; the commander identifies an unseen hazard; someone claims coercion; an injured person cannot follow instructions; one person carries a second credential or device; a spontaneous statement conflicts with Julie’s attribution; the package changes state after separation; or political actors pressure the RCMP to classify everyone as hostile.

Legitimate alternatives remain live: the commander may have intended surrender; the package may have been preserved rather than used; custodians may lack knowledge or act under duress; a technical act may be defensive; a person may be attempting to expose the package; the commander’s account may contain both truth and deception; and apparent flight may result from fear, injury, confusion, or an attempt to protect someone.

Continuity locks preserved

Canada retains territorial and operational authority. RCMP co-leadership remains substantive. United States assistance remains requested and bounded. Decisions 133 through 138 retain their proof ceilings. The original package remains under the phased Canadian-controlled protocol. Julie remains an evidentiary adviser rather than commander, custodian, arresting officer, or interrogator. Elias remains a supervised expert without unofficial access. The system remains non-sentient. The incomplete package does not become automatic proof of guilt, intent, conspiracy, operability, attack, state sponsorship, or wider command control.

Proof ceiling: The differentiated custody framework authorizes the RCMP to control the surrender scene, separate people from the package, and place each person under only the legal status supported by individualized Canadian evidence and authority. It does not make surrender an admission, make flight or package proximity proof of guilt, establish common purpose among custodians, prove package operability or harmful intent, connect the commander to the Decision 131 parent actor or the Western Pacific chain, or authorize indefinite detention, coercive interrogation, collective punishment, or United States custody in Canada.

Forward target: Decision 140 — permissible force, weapons, tactical-response, medical-duty, and disengagement boundaries during the RCMP climax operation.

## Decision 140 — An Integrated Force-and-Disengagement Matrix with an Independent Deadly-Force Threshold Governs the RCMP Climax Operation

Selection: D.

Status: Locked.

Decision

The RCMP climax operation uses an integrated, continuously reassessed force-and-disengagement matrix with an independent deadly-force threshold. Every action must have a currently lawful Canadian objective, a current factual basis, necessity after reasonable alternatives are considered, and proportionality to the immediate conduct and danger.

Decision 136 authorizes intervention to begin but does not determine the level of force. Decision 137 remains continuously operative and requires pause, narrowing, or cessation when authentication, legal authority, safety, surrender, or the active trigger changes.

Category 1 — Presence, positioning, barriers, and communication

The RCMP may use officer positioning, physical perimeters, protected observation, civilian and traffic control, evacuation, barriers, surrender instructions, negotiation, time and distance, authorized technical isolation, and protected medical access.

A visible surrender route is preserved whenever conditions permit.

Category 2 — Non-force physical intervention

Officers may block a route, shield another person, remove an unattended obstruction, or establish area control without intentionally applying force to a person. This category does not authorize touching, restraining, striking, or incapacitating a person.

Category 3 — Proportionate non-deadly force

Non-deadly force may be used only when reasonably necessary for an individualized lawful Canadian objective and lesser practical measures are insufficient.

Qualifying objectives may include protecting a person from injury; completing a lawful arrest or detention; controlling active physical resistance; separating a person from the package; stopping a currently authenticated Decision 136 action; preventing escape from established lawful custody; or preventing immediate evidence destruction when the selected method does not create a greater life or package hazard.

Force is directed only at the person whose conduct supports it. One custodian’s resistance does not authorize force against the group.

Passive presence, silence, a request for counsel, refusal to answer, confusion, fear, medical impairment, technical disagreement, or proximity to the package is not resistance by itself.

Category 4 — Deadly force

Deadly force is governed by an independent threshold. It is authorized only when competent Canadian personnel reasonably believe that the targeted person presents an imminent threat of death or serious bodily harm and deadly force is necessary to stop that threat.

Commander identity, a Decision 136 trigger, package possession or movement, evidence destruction, escape, refusal to surrender, refusal to provide credentials, political importance, suspected state affiliation, uncertainty about package operability, or fear that the investigation may fail never independently authorize deadly force.

An ambiguous package action cannot be treated as lethal merely because the package might be dangerous. The immediate life-threat conclusion requires its own factual basis.

Warnings and surrender

Before using force, the RCMP gives clear instructions, a surrender opportunity, and a reasonable chance to comply when doing so does not materially increase danger or defeat a necessary protective action.

Warnings may be shortened or omitted only when delay would materially increase an imminent threat to life or serious bodily safety. Apparent noncompliance is assessed in context, including the person’s ability to hear, understand, and physically comply.

Package-directed tactical actions

Any action directed at the package, its vehicle, connected equipment, or surrounding infrastructure requires separate Canadian hazard authorization.

Covered actions include firing at the package or a connected object; forcibly disabling a vehicle carrying it; destructive electronic interference; explosive or destructive entry; deliberate power disruption; actions likely to damage storage media; or actions capable of initiating an unknown package response.

Package-directed action is not authorized merely to preserve evidence or prevent escape. The incident commander must consider whether it could create greater danger, destroy the custody record, cause an unknown transmission, or defeat Decision 138’s stabilization protocol.

Mandatory narrowing and cessation

Force immediately narrows or stops when the person complies; resistance ends; the Decision 136 trigger ceases; surrender becomes effective; the person is restrained or reliably controlled; the person becomes incapacitated; authentication materially fails; Canadian authority ends or narrows; civilian or responder danger exceeds approved tolerance; the force creates an unacceptable package hazard; the lawful objective is achieved; or a medical emergency changes the operation’s required form.

An earlier valid justification cannot be banked after circumstances change. Renewed force requires a current factual and legal basis.

Medical duty

Preservation of life overrides capture, questioning, and evidence recovery.

When conditions permit, the RCMP assesses injured persons, requests appropriate medical support, permits urgent treatment, records injuries and observed causes, preserves evidence without delaying necessary care, reassesses restraint, and transfers medical custody under documented conditions.

Medical care cannot be conditioned on cooperation, credentials, statements, or consent to a package search. An injured commander or custodian remains subject only to the individualized status supported under Decision 139.

Canadian and United States boundary

United States personnel remain outside the Canadian tactical perimeter and do not conduct the operation.

They may not select targets, authorize force, direct RCMP positioning, operate weapons or tactical systems, pursue a person in Canada, disable the package, or convert technical advice into operational command.

An unavoidable personal emergency involving a United States participant is a separate protective incident and does not create United States participation in the RCMP operation.

Julie O’Donnell

Julie may report authentication failure, contradictory evidence, mistaken-identity risk, loss of commander-package continuity, an alternative explanation affecting the factual basis, or a mismatch between observed conduct and the asserted trigger.

She may not authorize force, designate a target, direct tactical movement, interpret silence as danger, or argue that the case’s importance justifies a higher force level. She remains outside the tactical command chain.

Her decisive contribution may be stopping an unjustified escalation when the evidence no longer supports the assumed danger.

Elias Thorne

Elias may warn the Canadian incident commander about technical behavior creating a potential life hazard; an action likely to damage or activate the package; a mistaken interpretation of routine technical behavior; indicators of automation, shielding, instability, or decoy substitution; or the likely consequences of a proposed package-directed action.

He may not direct force, manipulate the package remotely, designate a person as dangerous, provide unofficial credentials, or become the sole basis for a deadly-force conclusion.

His decisive contribution may be warning that an apparently protective tactical action would destabilize or destroy the package.

Recording and accountability

The RCMP records the lawful objective, observed conduct, warnings or reasons for omission, alternatives considered, force category, authorization and application, changes in factual basis, narrowing or cessation, injuries and medical response, effects on the package and evidence scene, and contradictions reported by Julie or Elias.

Force, medical, package-custody, and investigative records remain distinct but cross-referenced.

Narrative and structural consequences

The climax may escalate sharply while remaining governed. The RCMP can use bounded physical control, disengage when a trigger ends, resume only on current evidence and authority, and confront a genuine life threat without converting every package action into an attempted attack.

Decision 140 completes the operational stack:

Decision 136 controls the intervention trigger.

Decision 137 controls abort, pause, and narrowing.

Decision 138 controls package stabilization and examination.

Decision 139 controls individualized human custody.

Decision 140 controls the force used to carry out those authorities.

This permits a credible action climax while preserving the distinction between operational success and proof.

Suspense and alternatives

The scene may include partial surrender, individualized resistance, injury-limited compliance, a tool mistaken for a weapon, ambiguous vehicle movement, civilian intrusion, conflicting technical advice, authentication failure after force begins, or political pressure for escalation.

Apparent resistance may arise from panic, injury, communication failure, coercion, or protection of another person. Apparent weapon or package movement may be technical stabilization. Vehicle movement may be escape, evacuation, surrender, or medical transport.

Proof ceiling: The force-and-disengagement matrix authorizes only the level of Canadian force reasonably necessary for a currently lawful objective, while deadly force requires an independently reasonable belief of imminent death or serious bodily harm and all force must cease or narrow when its factual, legal, or safety basis ends. The use of force does not prove criminal guilt, harmful intent, package operability, an attempted attack, common purpose, a relationship with the Decision 131 parent actor, control of the Western Pacific chain, or state sponsorship, and it does not authorize punishment, evidence-driven deadly force, United States tactical action in Canada, or continued force against a compliant, restrained, incapacitated, or medically endangered person.

Forward target: Decision 141 — the first authenticated post-recovery finding produced by the incomplete portable package and how that bounded finding changes the joint investigation.

## Decision 141 — The First Authenticated Package Finding Establishes a Bounded Dual-Purpose Handoff with a Deliberate Evidentiary Gap

Selection: D.

Status: Locked.

Architecture lock

The first authenticated result produced under Decision 138 is a bounded dual-purpose handoff finding with a deliberate evidentiary gap. The recovered object is neither treated as a simple weapon nor reduced to a passive archive. It is established as a command-validation and evidence-preservation appliance capable of receiving a bounded instruction package, validating authority and integrity, preserving a receipt state, and supporting a separately installed execution, transmission, routing, or record component.

The Canadian forensic examination establishes five linked findings.

1. Authentic package lineage

A defined set of hardware, secure-element, firmware, and record characteristics is authentic to the relevant system family. The validated indicators may include secure-element identity, immutable boot measurements, authenticated firmware lineage, trusted-component relationships, system-family validation keys, known command-envelope structures, and manufacturing or deployment certificates.

This establishes that the package is not merely an improvised imitation or an ordinary container carrying copied labels. It does not establish that every recovered component shares one origin, that the package remained unchanged throughout its history, or that the package was complete when recovered.

2. Recent bounded operational handoff

The package received, validated, or generated one discrete operational exchange during a defined period connected to the climax. Surviving records establish a limited instruction or authorization bundle, integrity validation, authority-level classification, a package-generated acknowledgement or receipt, creation of an internal custody or handoff state, or a transition from one defined package state to another.

The finding proves that an operationally consequential handoff occurred. It does not establish the full instruction content, final destination, intended target, execution result, or harmful purpose.

3. Commander-level interaction or custody state

The package retains evidence consistent with interaction at the authority level associated with the authenticated commander or with physical custody controlled through that role. The surviving evidence may include a commander-class session, use of a commander-level device, a validated custody acknowledgement, a locally authenticated package-state transition, or a role-bound handoff record.

This materially supports commander-level interaction or custody. It does not prove who physically entered a command, whether the commander personally operated the package, whether the commander understood the underlying instruction, or whether another person used delegated, stolen, replayed, or automated authority.

4. Material evidentiary component absent or deliberately separated

The system architecture shows that a material execution, transmission, routing, or record component should ordinarily be associated with the validated handoff. That component is not present in the recovered evidence.

The absence is not treated as ordinary corruption or a merely unreadable sector. It presents as physical removal of a modular component, clean separation at an intended interface, deliberate cryptographic severance, deletion confined to a defined record class, removal of a separately held execution token, absence of a companion transmission module, or a custody break preserved by the package itself.

The package may authenticate that separation occurred and may bound the latest period in which the relationship existed. It does not reliably establish who separated the component, why it was separated, where it went, whether it was copied, or whether it was ever installed in the recovered configuration.

5. Bounded Western Pacific technical marker

One surviving lineage or interoperability marker is consistent with the technical environment already associated with the Western Pacific investigation. The marker may consist of a shared protocol family, validation namespace, component profile, routing grammar, test-certificate lineage, or command-envelope format.

The marker establishes a bounded technical relationship or interoperability possibility. It does not prove communication with a particular Western Pacific actor, common authorship, common control, shared hostile purpose, one parent authority, or state sponsorship.

The evidentiary gap is a finding, not a presumption of guilt

The missing component is recorded as an authenticated investigative fact. Its absence is not automatically attributed to the commander or any custodian and is not automatically characterized as concealment, destruction, obstruction, or evidence of an attempted attack.

The investigation separately records:

- the architecture that expected or supported the component;

- the evidence establishing its absence at recovery;

- the evidence indicating deliberate or designed separation;

- the latest authenticated period in which the package can establish the component’s presence or relationship;

- every person and location supported by independent evidence as having possible access;

- benign and hostile explanations for the separation;

- examiner confidence, limitations, and dissent.

Live alternative explanations include normal modular security practice, separate lawful custody, removal by another custodian, transfer before the commander obtained the package, safety isolation, evidence preservation, destruction, copying followed by removal, an intentionally incomplete package design, emergency stabilization effects, or planned later surrender.

What the first finding does not establish

The result does not establish:

- the full content of the validated instruction;

- the final destination or target;

- whether execution or transmission occurred;

- whether the package was fully operable at recovery;

- who removed or separated the missing component;

- whether the commander ordered, understood, opposed, or merely preserved the handoff;

- whether the missing element was destroyed, transferred, copied, concealed, preserved elsewhere, or never installed;

- whether the Decision 131 parent actor participated;

- whether one authority controls the Western Pacific chain;

- state sponsorship;

- an ordered or attempted attack;

- common criminal purpose among the commander and custodians.

Effect on the joint investigation

The finding validates continued investigation while narrowing its next questions. It supports focused preservation requests, individualized status review under Decision 139, applications for separately grounded search authority, examination of custodian roles, bounded Canadian–United States information sharing, and a protected joint finding.

It does not create unlimited search authority against every custodian, associate, residence, workplace, device, account, or location. Any search for the missing component requires evidence connecting the component to a person, place, device, repository, route, or transfer event.

Canadian and United States repositories remain distinct. Canada preserves the original package, recovery record, acquisition, examiner logs, authenticated artifacts, excluded material, and findings. The United States receives only approved derivative reports and supporting records under the existing sharing rules.

Character and institutional consequences

Julie’s central post-climax duty is to state the finding precisely and prevent political or institutional actors from converting it into a public claim that a complete weapon was recovered, an attack was foiled, or the parent actor issued a direct order.

Elias may explain why the missing component matters, identify expected interfaces, and describe testable architectural alternatives. He may not recreate missing contents from design knowledge, use undisclosed access, or become the sole source of a critical inference.

The commander’s legal and narrative status remains individualized and contested. The finding may support continued custody or investigation when combined with separate evidence, but it also leaves the commander able to claim that the component was removed for protection, surrender, compartmentalization, preservation, or reasons unrelated to harmful use. That account must be tested rather than automatically accepted or rejected.

Narrative and series function

The book receives a concrete forensic payoff: the package is genuine, a bounded operational handoff occurred, commander-level interaction or custody is materially supported, a bounded Western Pacific relationship exists, and a crucial component is demonstrably missing.

The package does not become a confession, complete attack record, automatic exoneration, or complete conspiracy map. The unresolved question is precise and actionable: who controlled the missing component, where it went, and what lawful evidence can establish its function and final consequence.

Proof ceiling: The first forensic finding establishes that the recovered package contained authentic components and records consistent with a dual-purpose command-validation and evidence-preservation system, that it participated in a bounded recent operational handoff, and that a material execution, transmission, or record component was absent or deliberately separated before recovery. It does not prove who removed or separated that component, who authored or ordered the underlying activity, that the commander understood or intended harmful use, that the package was fully operable, that an attack was ordered or attempted, or that the package connects the commander to the Decision 131 parent actor, control of the Western Pacific chain, or state sponsorship.

Forward target: Decision 142 — the lawful search, preservation, and recovery strategy for the missing execution, transmission, or record component, including how persons and locations are prioritized without converting absence into guilt.

## Decision 142 — An Evidence-Led Priority Ladder Governs the Missing Component Search, Preservation, and Recovery

Selection: D.

Status: Locked.

The joint investigation pursues the missing execution, transmission, or record component through a staged evidence-led priority ladder. The architecture separates preservation, lead development, priority classification, search authorization, recovery and forensic comparison, and status reassessment. The component’s absence creates a focused investigative question; it does not create general authority to search everyone or everything associated with the authenticated commander.

Governing distinctions

Preservation maintains evidence at risk. It does not authorize access, content review, search, seizure, or exploitation.

Association does not establish individualized linkage. Access opportunity does not establish possession. Recovery does not establish authentication. One jurisdiction’s authority does not transfer automatically to another.

Stage 1 — Immediate bounded preservation

Investigators identify records genuinely at risk of routine deletion, overwrite, loss, transfer, or destruction. Specifically bounded preservation may cover surveillance recordings, access-control records, transportation and border records, vehicle records, communications metadata, service-provider connection records, cloud or repository records, shipping and procurement records, employer or facility logs, storage records, and relevant device-management records.

Every preservation request identifies the component-related factual basis, relevant time window, record category, target person, place, device, account, or facility, risk of loss, and governing jurisdiction.

Broad preservation of every record belonging to every associate is prohibited. Preservation does not authorize investigators to read content, access an account, search a device, or seize property.

Stage 2 — Component custody-and-opportunity matrix

The investigation builds a non-conclusive matrix identifying where the component could have been separated, transferred, stored, copied, concealed, or destroyed.

The matrix may use the last authenticated package state showing the component relationship; physical interface residue; validated timestamps; recovery-scene evidence; custody and transport records; camera, access, vehicle, facility, border, and route records; lawful device-pairing evidence; verified communications or meetings; relevant repository events; documented statements; physical dimensions and storage requirements; and transparent technical requirements established through the Decision 138 examination process.

Every entry is classified as authenticated fact, corroborated inference, uncorroborated allegation, unresolved contradiction, or benign alternative explanation.

No person or place enters the matrix solely because of friendship, employment, family connection, political affiliation, shared nationality, shared organization, generic communication, or proximity to the commander.

Stage 3 — Evidence-based priority tiers

Tier 1 — Direct authenticated linkage

A Tier 1 lead contains evidence directly connecting a specific person, place, device, account, repository, route, or transfer event to the missing component. Examples include a component-specific identifier, authenticated pairing evidence, a verified transfer record, video or access evidence showing a compatible object, a package record naming a specific interface or repository, a corroborated statement supported by independent evidence, or sufficiently distinctive physical residue or container evidence.

Tier 1 may support immediate application for the appropriate authority. It does not establish guilt or harmful purpose.

Tier 2 — Corroborated access and opportunity

A Tier 2 lead has at least two independent evidence streams supporting meaningful access and opportunity during the relevant separation period. The evidence may combine physical access, verified presence, technical capability, relevant communication, unusual transfer behavior, and matching transport or storage evidence.

Tier 2 supports focused investigation and may support a warrant only when the applicable legal standard is independently met.

Tier 3 — Contextual investigative lead

A Tier 3 lead is plausible but lacks direct or sufficiently corroborated linkage. It may support voluntary inquiry, lawful public-record review, narrowly bounded preservation when loss risk and authority exist, or comparison against evidence already lawfully held. Tier 3 does not justify an intrusive search merely because the component is important.

Excluded association category

A person or location is excluded from search priority when the connection rests only on family or professional association, shared organization, political disagreement, presence in an address book, generic communications, ordinary travel, nationality, common technical similarity, or proximity without evidence of access.

Stage 4 — Separate authority for every search

Every physical or digital search requires authority tied to a specifically identified target; evidence connecting that target to the missing component; the place, device, account, data, or repository to be searched; the evidence expected to be found; the relevant period; the proposed method; minimization and scope limits; and the governing jurisdiction.

Canadian authorities govern Canadian persons, places, seizures, devices, accounts, and repositories. United States authorities govern American targets through separate American process. Western Pacific partners receive only approved information and must independently establish their authority.

No jurisdiction may rely on another jurisdiction’s warrant, detention, seizure, or investigative label as a substitute for its own lawful basis.

Stage 5 — Independent and narrow emergency authority

Emergency action may occur only when current evidence establishes an independently recognized emergency, such as imminent danger to life, an active destructive process, immediate transfer beyond practical lawful reach, or a technically authenticated risk of irreversible evidence loss.

The component’s importance, political pressure, institutional embarrassment, or uncertainty about a possible attack does not independently create emergency authority.

Emergency action stops when the emergency ends. Continued examination or exploitation requires ordinary lawful authority.

Stage 6 — Recovery and comparison protocol

Any candidate recovered in Canada enters a separate Decision 138-compliant custody and forensic process.

Investigators document the recovery location, condition, and independent custody history; stabilize only as necessary; avoid connecting the candidate directly to the recovered package; obtain authority for examination; acquire and analyze verified working copies where possible; and compare authenticated characteristics through documented methods.

The recovered package and candidate originals are not physically or digitally merged merely to test whether they function together. A successful cryptographic, physical, component, or record relationship is documented as a comparison result without erasing the originals’ separate custody histories.

A foreign recovery remains in that jurisdiction’s custody. Approved derivative findings may be shared, but the Canadian repository does not absorb a foreign original.

Stage 7 — Negative and contradictory results have operational force

An unsuccessful search is not evidence that a person concealed the component elsewhere.

A negative result must reduce or remove the target’s priority when appropriate, enter the joint evidence record, reach decision-makers relying on the earlier lead, trigger review of related detention, arrest, surveillance, or search positions, and prevent the disproven theory from being repeated in another jurisdiction without new evidence.

Contradictions identified by Julie, Elias, examiners, custodians, counsel, or independent reviewers are preserved rather than filtered out of the lead package.

Stage 8 — Commander and custodian status review

Decision 141 does not automatically strengthen or extend every person’s custody status.

The missing-component investigation may affect a person only when evidence specific to that individual supports possession, transfer, concealment, destruction, technical control, a materially relevant false statement, or another legally meaningful act.

The missing component cannot justify indefinite detention while investigators search for evidence. Exculpatory, protective, or benign evidence must be incorporated into the Decision 139 status review.

Julie’s role

Julie audits the provenance of every priority claim and maintains the distinction between component absence, access opportunity, possession, concealment, harmful purpose, and higher-actor attribution.

She challenges recycled or circular evidence, identifies when one jurisdiction’s allegation has become another jurisdiction’s supposed corroboration, records alternatives, and requires negative results to flow back into the joint assessment.

Julie cannot authorize searches or convert analytic confidence into legal authority.

Elias’s role

Elias may define transparent, testable component characteristics; identify physical and technical storage requirements; explain expected interfaces; distinguish meaningful matches from generic compatibility; and identify search or comparison methods that could activate, alter, or destroy evidence.

He may not use private credentials, remotely probe possible systems, conduct unsupervised comparisons, identify a target from intuition alone, or become the sole source for a warrant’s critical factual premise.

Narrative and structural effect

The missing component creates a focused procedural pursuit rather than an indiscriminate manhunt.

The denouement may produce a direct Tier 1 transfer, a Tier 2 custodian whose role changes after corroboration, a technical lead pointing away from the commander, an urgent preservation race, a negative search requiring release or reclassification, evidence that the component left Canada before the climax, or a protected intermediary holding it for reasons not yet understood.

The book may close without full recovery because the investigation now knows what it is looking for, what evidence can prioritize a lead, what authority is required, what cannot justify a search, how a candidate will be tested, and how negative results must change the investigation.

Suspense arises from expiring surveillance records, routine deletion, a storage clearance, a vehicle or courier nearing a border, a Tier 1 identifier appearing elsewhere, an incompletely corroborated disclosure, a possible decoy, or political pressure for simultaneous broad searches. The tension comes from obtaining the right evidence and authority before the trail disappears, not from bypassing them.

Legitimate alternatives remain live

The component may have been separated as routine modular security practice, transferred lawfully, isolated for safety, preserved by a whistleblower, held by someone unaware of its significance, removed before the commander obtained the package, copied and then destroyed, never installed, stored in another jurisdiction, embedded in ordinary equipment, or divided across multiple records or devices.

The priority ladder tests hostile and benign explanations without selecting one prematurely.

Proof ceiling: The evidence-led priority ladder authorizes preservation, investigation, search, seizure, and recovery actions only against specifically identified persons, places, devices, accounts, repositories, routes, or transfer events supported by the evidence and authority applicable to that action and jurisdiction. It does not make the component’s absence proof of concealment, make access opportunity proof of possession, make an unsuccessful search evidence of guilt, establish that a recovered candidate is the missing component without authenticated comparison, or prove harmful intent, package operability, an attempted attack, parent-actor involvement, control of the Western Pacific chain, common command, or state sponsorship.

Forward target: Decision 143 — the bounded end-of-book result of the missing-component pursuit, including what is recovered or preserved, what remains unresolved, and how that result positions the final character and architecture-closure decisions.

## Decision 143 — Partial Canadian Recovery Establishes a Tier 1 Post-Separation Route While the Operational Core Remains Unrecovered

Selection: B.

Status: Locked.

Book 2 ends with a partial but consequential recovery. Canadian authorities recover a physically authentic, non-operational interface-and-record segment that formed part of the missing component. The segment retains a component-specific identifier and limited transfer-state information, but the execution, transmission, routing, or operational core remains unrecovered and may be outside Canadian reach.

Three independently preserved evidence streams establish the result: the package-derived component identifier and interface profile; the physical and technical comparison between that profile and the recovered segment; and a source-native custody receipt connecting the segment to a defined Canadian transfer event and outbound route. Together they create a Tier 1 relationship to the recovered segment and the documented post-separation transfer. Evidence beyond the last authenticated handoff remains Tier 2 at most, and no final recipient or destination is treated as established.

The authenticated sequence places the separation and first transfer before the commander’s presently established package-custody interval. It therefore points away from the commander as the direct post-separation possessor and away from at least one immediate custodian previously treated as a concealment suspect. It does not establish whether the commander knew the package was incomplete, participated earlier, received derivative information, or had any relationship to the later route.

Negative and contradictory evidence must change the outcome. At least one immediate custodian is released or reclassified as a voluntary or protected witness because the corrected chronology and negative searches exclude that person from the authenticated post-separation route. A Canadian intermediary, transfer actor, account, repository event, or logistics record directly tied to the authenticated transfer may become the subject of focused preservation, production, interview, or search process. No arrest, detention, or intrusive search follows without individualized evidence and the governing Canadian authority.

Canada retains the recovered segment, its independent custody history, the original custody receipt, the recovered package, the package-derived comparison material, and all Canadian forensic originals. The segment and package remain physically and digitally separate under the Decision 138 controls. The United States receives only an approved route notice, verified hashes or images of the non-operational segment, bounded component characteristics, and the last authenticated transfer window. Any American person, device, account, carrier, repository, or place requires separate American authority. A Western Pacific partner receives only approved information and independently determines whether its own evidence and law support action.

The ending is delivered through authentication of the custody receipt, lawful recovery and comparison of the non-operational segment, and an individualized status review compelled by the corrected chronology—not through a second tactical climax. The material result gives Book 2 a concrete answer: the missing component followed a real, defined post-separation path, the original commander-centered search was materially incomplete, and an authentic portion of the component is now preserved in Canada.

Julie identifies that two supposedly independent claims against the over-targeted custodian originated from the same unverified allegation. She preserves the contradictory chronology, requires negative results to affect custody and priority, and separates the authenticated Canadian route from speculative foreign attribution. She does not authorize searches, determine charges, or convert analytical confidence into legal authority.

Elias defines transparent tests showing that the recovered piece is a distinctive component segment rather than generic compatible hardware. He also explains why connecting it to the package could alter evidence or create a false functional result. Independent Canadian examiners perform and document the comparison. Elias does not connect the segment to the package, use private credentials, remotely probe downstream systems, or become the sole basis for authentication or process.

Legitimate alternatives remain open. The operational core may have been protectively separated, preserved by a frightened insider, transferred under coercion, placed in unauthorized escrow, deliberately concealed, stolen during transport, destroyed, copied, or moved by someone who did not understand its function. The bounded series-level continuation is limited to who controlled the operational core after the last authenticated handoff, why it was moved, and whether later evidence can establish a lawful route, destination, intent, or higher actor.

Proof ceiling: Partial recovery and authenticated transfer evidence establish only that the recovered segment formed part of the component missing from the Canadian package and that a component-specific object followed the documented post-separation path through the last authenticated transfer event. They do not establish delivery beyond that event, identify who controlled the unrecovered operational core, establish who ordered or understood the separation, prove that the commander possessed or directed the missing core, prove harmful intent, component or package operability, activation, transmission, use, or an attempted attack, establish Decision 131 parent-actor involvement, establish control of the Western Pacific chain, establish common command, or establish state sponsorship.

Forward target: Decision 144 — Julie’s final professional, institutional, and personal position after the corrected investigation, including her authority boundaries, institutional consequences, relationship to the protected record, and the choice that carries her into the series continuation.

## Decision 144 — Julie Accepts an Independent, Time-Limited Canadian Evidentiary-Integrity Review Mandate

Selection: B.

Status: Locked.

Core position: Julie declines both an ordinary return to her former reporting chain and a permanent senior promotion. She accepts a formally constituted, time-limited Canadian evidentiary-integrity review mandate focused on the failures exposed during Book 2 and on the protected continuation of the missing-component inquiry. The mandate reports to a designated Canadian reviewing authority outside the immediate operational chain and is bounded by identified subject matter, records, and questions rather than a general power to investigate anything associated with the case.

Mandate and authorities: Julie may review the provenance of Canadian-held evidence and approved derivative records; test whether supposedly independent claims share a common source; preserve contradictions, minority assessments, negative results, and benign alternatives; examine whether corrected chronologies changed custody, surveillance, search, and priority decisions; audit how cross-border claims retained or lost their original confidence and limitations; issue protected findings and recommendations; and refer apparent legal, professional, security, or evidentiary concerns to the body that possesses the proper authority. She may not compel testimony, issue process, search or seize property, direct tactical operations, order detention or release, decide charges, authorize forensic examination, declassify information, conduct intelligence exploitation, or direct a foreign partner.

Narrative and personal consequence: Julie receives a meaningful institutional consequence from the climax without receiving implausibly broad power. The institution cannot simply return her to the same position or erase the correction she forced, but she does not abandon public service or claim that the entire system is irredeemable. Her choice is to remain engaged only through declared authority, preserved dissent, reviewable methods, and explicit limits. She will not perform off-book work, rely on undeclared access, or allow urgency to erase the distinction between an analytical judgment and lawful authority.

Character position: The choice completes Julie’s Book 2 movement from an analyst operating inside an inherited structure to a professional whose integrity has acquired an explicit institutional form. She accepts responsibility rather than comfort, while reserving the right to document disagreement and decline work that exceeds the mandate. Her continued service is therefore neither unconditional reconciliation nor total institutional withdrawal.

Structural consequence: The mandate gives Julie sufficient lawful access and mobility for later books while preventing her from becoming a standing law-enforcement commander. It may follow defined evidentiary questions across Canadian agencies and approved joint records, but it does not create universal access or an independent operational service. The substantive review can continue in later architecture; Book 2 needs only the bounded offer, Julie’s conditions of acceptance, and preservation of the corrected record.

Procedural and jurisdictional limits: Julie’s access is restricted to records expressly included in the mandate or lawfully provided to it. The recovered Canadian package, recovered segment, Canadian forensic originals, foreign-held material, United States repositories, and Western Pacific records remain under their respective custody and legal regimes. Canadian bodies within scope must preserve responsive records and provide a written disposition of formal recommendations. They may accept, reject, narrow, or defer a recommendation, but may not erase the underlying finding or represent silence as agreement. Foreign material remains subject to the supplying jurisdiction’s conditions, and Julie may audit how a foreign claim was represented after entering the Canadian record without demanding foreign records or directing foreign activity.

Protected record: The corrected chronology, duplicated-source finding, negative searches, status corrections, and Julie’s bounded assessments remain in an identifiable protected annex. Later institutional summaries may describe or contest those findings, but may not silently rewrite Julie’s recorded analysis. Public disclosure, redaction, litigation access, source protection, and final repository disposition remain for the later institutional-consequences decision.

Elias boundary and relationship: Elias does not work privately for Julie and does not become her technical arm. Julie may formulate documented technical questions or recommend independent testing, but the relevant Canadian authority separately decides whether work is authorized, who performs it, what materials are accessible, how conflicts are managed, and how the result is preserved. Any later Elias appointment, supervision, disclosure duties, and access limits are established separately in Decision 145. Personally, Julie does not sever the relationship, but she refuses to make it an evidentiary shortcut: no undeclared exchanges, private credentials, unofficial testing, or off-record transfer of restricted information.

Suspense and legitimate alternatives: The mandate may be sincere reform, an institutional effort to regain credibility, a controlled experiment in independent review, a way of keeping Julie close enough to monitor, or several of those things at once. Different Canadian and foreign actors may cooperate, lawfully contest scope, protect legitimate sources and methods, slow review for institutional reasons, or resist for self-interested reasons. Resistance therefore creates suspense without automatically proving corruption. The unresolved operational core preserves the external series question, while the mandate preserves the institutional question of whether correction survives renewed political pressure.

Proof ceiling: Julie’s independent review mandate, protected access, preserved findings, and authority to issue recommendations establish only that Canada has created a bounded mechanism for examining identified evidentiary-integrity questions and that Julie has the defined professional standing to perform that review. They do not establish that every Julie assessment is correct, confer search, seizure, detention, charging, tactical, forensic, intelligence, declassification, or foreign-command authority, require another institution or jurisdiction to adopt her conclusions, establish bad faith or criminal misconduct by anyone whose work is reviewed, prove complete innocence or guilt of any commander or custodian, or prove the missing core’s destination, operability, harmful intent, use, attempted attack, parent-actor involvement, Western Pacific control, common command, or state sponsorship.

Forward target: Decision 145 — Elias’s final professional and investigative status, including any independent technical-audit role, his supervision and accountability boundaries, his relationship to Julie’s mandate and the joint investigation, and the choice that carries him into the series continuation.

## Decision 145 — Elias Accepts a Separate, Time-Limited Independent Canadian Technical-Audit Appointment

Selection: B.

Status: Locked.

Core position. Elias accepts a formally documented, time-limited Canadian appointment as an independent technical auditor. His appointment is separate from Julie’s evidentiary-integrity review mandate and separate from the operational, custodial, prosecutorial, intelligence, and foreign-liaison chains. It addresses defined technical-integrity questions arising from Book 2 and the protected continuation of the missing-component inquiry. It does not make Elias a member of the investigative command, Julie’s subordinate, the package’s operator, or the technical owner of the case.

Independent accountability lines. Elias’s work has two separate accountability lines. A designated Canadian forensic-science lead, laboratory authority, or independent technical-review chair approves each work plan, controls the examination environment, and verifies that the methods are safe, reproducible, and within scope. The Canadian authority responsible for Julie’s mandate receives Elias’s protected reports, preserves dissent and limitations, and determines whether a recommendation must be referred to an institution possessing operational, legal, custodial, or foreign-relations authority. Neither line may privately direct Elias to reach a predetermined conclusion.

Authorized technical-audit remit. Within separately approved assignments, Elias may audit whether technical conclusions are reproducible from preserved data; distinguish component-specific characteristics from generic compatibility; review whether an identifier, interface remnant, container artifact, routing record, recovered segment, or derivative record was accurately characterized; examine whether timestamps, logs, derived records, or comparison outputs were transformed or represented beyond their validated limits; identify when a proposed comparison could activate, alter, overwrite, merge, or destroy evidence; assess whether a technical claim depends on an undocumented tool, assumption, credential, source, or common underlying record; evaluate whether supposedly independent technical claims derive from the same source; document benign, non-operational, contamination, maintenance, testing, or third-party explanations; recommend replication, peer review, preservation, or a differently constituted examination; and issue a protected dissent when the available material does not support the institutional conclusion.

Authority exclusions. Elias may not decide which person should be searched, detained, charged, released, protected, or publicly identified. He may not select evidence for seizure, direct interviews, control tactical activity, compel disclosure, classify or declassify information, negotiate foreign access, or independently enlarge an examination because a new lead appears promising. His appointment supplies technical-review authority only within each written assignment.

Access and method safeguards. Elias receives access only after the relevant Canadian record custodian and technical-audit authority approve the specific materials, purpose, method, environment, supervising officials, permitted outputs, and end point. Access is logged and terminates with the assignment. Where possible, he works from verified forensic working copies, documented extracts, reproducible test environments, preserved tool versions, and independently generated comparison outputs. Originals remain under their existing custody. Elias may observe or advise on work involving an original only when separately authorized and supervised.

No private credential, remembered access token, former administrative privilege, unofficial repository, personal device, or remote probing method may be used. Discovery of an unexpected credential or access path is documented and referred; it is not exercised by Elias on his own authority. He may not directly connect the recovered package, recovered segment, bounded interface artifact, or any candidate operational core merely to determine whether they function together. Any method that could activate, alter, overwrite, merge, or destroy evidence requires separate authorization and an independently approved safety protocol.

Narrative consequences. Elias receives a meaningful ending that matches Julie’s without duplicating it. Julie’s integrity becomes institutionally recognized through an evidentiary-review mandate. Elias’s expertise becomes institutionally useful only after it is separated from personal trust, improvisation, and unrestricted access. His character choice is to accept constraint as part of technical truth. He does not demand that the institution trust him because he was helpful or because Julie believes him. He accepts logging, supervision, replication, conflict disclosure, peer criticism, and the possibility that another examiner may overturn his conclusion.

This produces a mature resolution rather than a triumphant expert fantasy. Elias remains exceptional, but the architecture refuses to make exceptionality a legal or evidentiary authority. His continuing role is consequential because the surviving technical record remains incomplete, not because he becomes an unofficial system key.

Structural consequences. The appointment gives Elias a credible continuing role in the bounded series mystery without making him indispensable to every technical problem. It may examine the surviving interface artifact, partial recovery, technical methodology, derivative records, and future material lawfully supplied to Canada while the operational core remains unrecovered. Because the appointment is time-limited and question-specific, any extension, materially new assignment, or later-book appointment requires fresh justification, scope, conflict review, supervision, and access authority. Elias acquires no permanent repository access merely because the unresolved mystery continues.

The separate appointment also protects the Julie–Elias relationship from becoming the mechanism through which evidence moves. They can remain personally important to one another while their institutional work travels through different authorities, records, and review paths. Their relationship supplies neither automatic trust nor automatic disqualification.

Procedural and jurisdictional consequences. Elias’s appointment is Canadian. It governs only Canadian-held materials and foreign-derived records lawfully made available within Canada under the supplying jurisdiction’s conditions. For United States material, the United States decides whether to conduct its own examination, provide an approved derivative record, permit supervised access, or decline. Canadian authority cannot compel American repository access, and Elias cannot use Canadian access to reconstruct or enter an American system. For Western Pacific material, each partner retains custody and independently decides what may be supplied. Elias may audit how a supplied claim was represented in the Canadian record, but he cannot direct the foreign collection, examination, retention, or disclosure.

Canadian and foreign conclusions remain separately attributed. A foreign partner’s agreement does not authenticate Elias’s method, and Elias’s disagreement does not establish that the foreign partner acted improperly. If a foreign condition prevents meaningful replication, the limitation is preserved in the report rather than hidden or converted into certainty.

Relationship to Julie’s mandate. Julie may issue a documented referral identifying the evidentiary proposition that requires technical review. She may explain why the proposition matters and identify provenance, circularity, or contradiction concerns. She may not select the answer, approve Elias’s access, direct his technical method, suppress a result, edit his protected findings, or receive restricted findings through their personal relationship. Elias may not privately brief Julie before the protected report is entered into the record. Questions, clarifications, disagreements, and revisions are documented.

Their personal relationship is disclosed. Work directly evaluating Julie’s own conduct, representations, or handling of technical material is assigned to another examiner or independently replicated. Where the relationship creates a material conflict or appearance concern, the review authority may narrow Elias’s assignment, require a second auditor, or reassign it. The relationship is neither automatically disqualifying nor a basis for relaxed safeguards.
